| Stopping the cross-org watch waits for the current cycle while herdr hangs. Each herdr, agmsg, and `git status` call of the pulse gets its own `DriverCallTimeout` (10 s) cut from `context.Background()`, not from the loop's context (`evaluateCycle` in `internal/org/watch.go`), so a SIGINT in the middle of a cycle does not cancel the calls and make live seats look dead (false liveness ALERTs). In exchange, a SIGINT, a SIGTERM, or a `--stop` request takes effect only when the cycle ends, which can take the number of hanging calls × 10 s, and then until the watch has waited for its in-flight on-demand watcher calls (`WatchHooks.BeforeStop`: they are cancelled at once, except that a `--once` watch stopped by `--stop` lets them finish, up to about 60 s + 10 s); `ralph org watch --stop` gives up after 30 s with exit 1 and leaves its request in `watch-stop.json`, which the watch follows when that cycle ends (found by S3a of org-cross-watch). Since stage 8b-1 (org-director-wake) the same cycle also holds the director's wake (`WakeDirector` in `internal/org/director_wake.go`, after the inbox deadlines and before the pulse): its herdr calls (the three label checks, `pane send-text`, and `pane send-keys`, with 750 ms between the last two) and `[org.director].wake_cmd` each get their own `DriverCallTimeout` the same way, so a stop can also wait for them, up to about 50 s while herdr hangs or 10 s for a `wake_cmd` that does not return. Since stage 8b-2a (org-director-liveness) the director check (`CheckDirector` in `internal/org/director_check.go`, after the inbox deadlines and before the wake) holds the cycle the same way: for a `running` director in herdr, its `herdr agent get` and, when herdr has no such agent, the three label checks of its pane, and for a `starting` or launch_cmd director its `ps` read, each get their own `DriverCallTimeout`, so a stop can also wait for them, at most about 40 s while herdr hangs or 10 s for a `ps` that does not return, and twice that when `director.jsonl` recorded a change for the registration during the check and the check is made once more | With 30 seats whose `agent get` hangs, a stop takes about 5 minutes or more, and `--stop` returns exit 1 before the watch has stopped. An operator who then kills the pid leaves a `running` mark behind; `ralph status` still says `not running`, because no one holds `watch.lock`. A second SIGINT or SIGTERM ends a foreground watch at once (`runOrgWatchAll` gives the signals their default effect back after the first) | Chosen in S3a: a slow stop is safer than ALERTs about seats that are alive, and the plan's risk register already accepts that a cycle can stretch to seats × 10 s while herdr hangs | The first report of a stop that took too long, or the next change to how `evaluateCycle` bounds its calls: check the stop request and the loop's context between orgs, so a stop waits for one org's calls rather than every org's, or cancel the calls from the loop's context after a grace period | `docs/plans/archive/2026-10-11-org-cross-watch.md` (Progress checklist, S3a; Risks and mitigations); commits 996e5c48 (S3a) and 33f6383b (S3b, `--stop` and the signals); `docs/plans/archive/2026-10-18-org-director-wake.md` (D6; Risks and mitigations); `docs/plans/archive/2026-10-18-org-director-liveness.md` (Risks and mitigations) |

| A cross-org watch that dies while seats are running is not noticed by the leaders and seats, and nobody restarts it automatically. The watch gate (`Org.WatchGate`, built by `NewWatchGate` in `internal/org/watchgate.go`) is a point-in-time check that `ralph org spawn`, `ralph org start`, and a TASK `ralph org send` make before they begin work; it does not stop the seats already running. A watch that is killed, crashes, or hangs after the check leaves the seats working with no pulse ALERT, no deadman escalation, and no inbox deadline (since 8a-1, org-director-attach, an item escalated while the watch accepted work is only recorded, delivery `deadline`, and waits for a watch to send it at its ack deadline). Since stage 8b-2b (org-director-wait) a director that waits with `ralph org wait --inbox --director <id>` notices: the wait probes the watch every 5 s and exits 4 when a watch that accepted work no longer does and no item is open, also when the watch stopped between two waits (the previous mark's `watch_accepting` carries over), and the `/director` skill has the director tell the human what `watch.lock` and `watch-alive.json` show and the `ralph org watch --all --detach` that starts one. Still not noticed by a push-only director (the push wake is sent by the watch itself), a director that waits without `--director`, or the leaders, until the next spawn, start, or TASK meets the refusal (the leader then escalates a BLOCKED) or a human reads the `org watch:` line of `ralph status`. Neither the wait nor the director restarts the watch: its mark reads the same whether the human stopped it or it ended on its own, so only the human starts it again | Seats can keep working unwatched until a human starts a watch again: a stalled seat raises no ALERT, an unanswered leader no deadman escalation, and inbox items past their deadline are not sent, even after a director told the human. An item escalated after the watch died is not lost (with no watch accepting work, escalate tells the human at once, reason `inbox_no_watch`), but one escalated before, while the watch ran, waits unsent (the restarted watch sends it in its first cycle). A leader that sends no new TASK and spawns no seat never meets the refusal | FR-6 of `docs/specs/2026-10-07-org-multi-org-director.md` is read as "do not start work when the watch is known to be missing" (org-watch-gate, Assumptions). Stopping running seats when the watch disappears, a second process that watches the watch, or an automatic restart is new behavior with failure modes of its own. The director's notice moved from stage 8a to 8b-2 and from 8b-2a to 8b-2b, which added it to the `--director` wait only; by the consult's ruling on Codex's plan advice 1 it starts no watch (org-director-wait D6) | A later stage that adds a heartbeat check for the leaders (for example the leader's own loop probing `ProbeWatch` and escalating when the watch does not accept work), or the first report of seats that ran unwatched, or of an inbox item that waited past its ack deadline | `docs/plans/archive/2026-10-12-org-watch-gate.md` (Design decisions, the last item; Assumptions); `docs/plans/archive/2026-10-14-org-director-attach.md` (Non-goals; Risks); `docs/plans/archive/2026-10-18-org-director-liveness.md` (Non-goals); `docs/plans/archive/2026-10-19-org-director-wait.md` (D3, D6; Non-goals) |
| A `--stop` typed from another terminal can be outlived by a process that waits for `watch.lock`. While `ralph org watch --all --detach` or `ralph org start` waits for the child it launched to exit (`launchAndAwaitOrgWatch` in `internal/cli/org_watch.go`, up to `orgWatchChildExitWait`, 3 s), or while a foreground `ralph org watch --all` waits for the lock (`org.DefaultWatchLockWait`, 2 s), a `--stop` from another terminal stops the watch that holds the lock: that watch removes the stop request (`watch-stop.json`) and releases the lock (`stopAndRelease` in `internal/org/watch.go`), and the waiting process (the child that lost the lock race, or the foreground watch) then takes the lock and runs on as a long-running watch. That `--stop` waits 30 s (`orgWatchStopDeadline`) for the lock to be free and exits 1. The base dc41c4f5 does the same in the foreground case. A second manifestation was self-review C2-1, fixed in 3fb0fd1b: a child still alive at the end of `orgWatchChildExitWait` is stopped, which can take the rest of its lock wait (a SIGTERM does not end the wait of `AcquireWatchLock`), and the other watch can end meanwhile, so `--detach` said `already running (pid N)` for a watch that was gone. `launchAndAwaitOrgWatch` now probes again after that stop and returns a watch that accepts work if one remains; when none does, `--detach` and start refuse with "the watch (pid N) was stopped while another watch held <lock>, and that one has ended too, so no watch runs; see <watch.log>". No test or live check covers the `--stop` from another terminal; the C2-1 refusal is pinned only with a fake child (`TestOrgWatchDetach_AnotherWatchRuns_AStoppedChildIsJudgedByAProbeAfterTheStop`) | The operator who typed `--stop` gets exit 1 after 30 s and finds a watch with another pid running (`ralph status` says `running`); a second `--stop` stops it. Nothing is corrupted: the watch that runs on holds `watch.lock`, writes its mark, and follows the next stop request. A script that stops and starts the watch reads the exit 1 as a failed stop. The window is the lock wait of the waiting process (2 s; up to 3 s for `--detach` and start, which also wait for the child to exit); a `--stop` typed after those calls have returned does not meet it, since from fdfb5b23 on they return only once the child has exited | The plan of org-watch-gate chose to write the hole down, not to close it. The fixes that were weighed and set aside (fdfb5b23, with consult): a child lock wait of 0 (it races with the momentary shared lock of `ProbeWatch`) and keeping the stop request after the watch stops (it breaks the stop-request rule of 6a, `takeStopRequestAtStart` in `internal/org/watch.go`: a request not later than a watch's start is for an earlier watch and is removed). The base has the foreground case, so this PR does not make it worse; the other window it had opened, a `--stop` after concurrent `--detach` calls had returned, was closed by fdfb5b23 | The first report of a `--stop` that timed out with another watch left running, or the next change to `AcquireWatchLock`, `stopAndRelease`, or the stop request (`WriteWatchStopRequest`): for example make a process that waits for the lock look at the stop request when it gets the lock (and end if one was written while it waited), or have `--stop` wait for the pid it saw to end and then check that no other watch took the lock. | The pane check of `ralph org approve` is a tripwire, not a boundary. `approveTripwire` (`internal/org/approve.go`) refuses approve only when `HERDR_PANE_ID` is the pane of an active seat, so a leader that runs `env -u HERDR_PANE_ID ralph org approve <ID>` approves its own plan, and so does a leader that appends a `plan_approved` line to the manifest itself: the seats run as the same user and can write the ledger, and the approval check (`checkPlanApproved`) cannot tell such a line from one approve wrote. The approval check also reads only the role name `implementer` and the TASK's `PLAN:` header, not the work a seat does, so a seat of a custom role (`--role <name> --prompt ...`), a TASK to the reviewer, and a TASK whose `PLAN:` names an approved plan while its body asks for other work all go ahead without an approval. The spec's Security considerations say that neither the director nor the leader can get around the checks; plan A2 reads that as not met to the letter for the approval, and keeps who ran approve in the record instead (`by=` from USER, `herdr_pane=` set or unset, and the quoted `cwd=`) | A leader that does this, by mistake or on an injected instruction, starts its implementer on a plan no human saw. The leader template tells it never to run approve, and the record shows who ran approve and where in `ralph org report`'s event timeline; a line written by hand carries whatever its writer put there, so the audit shows only what was written | Nothing in a single-user ledger tells the human from the seats; the plan chose a record for the audit (user story 5 of the spec) over a boundary it cannot build (Design decisions A2, Assumptions) | Stage 8a (the director records the approval on the human's answer) or a need for a real boundary. 8a-1 (org-director-attach) has its interactive director run `ralph org approve` on the human's answer through the same tripwire and record, so that PR leaves this row as it is; the stage-8a part of the trigger moves to the headless director of stage 8a-2, which runs in a herdr workspace. The headless director of 8a-2 (org-director-start) runs `ralph org approve` on the human's answer from its herdr pane through the same tripwire and record: the tripwire does not refuse that pane, since the director is not a seat and has no line in the manifest (D2 of that plan), and the record carries `herdr_pane=` set, so that PR leaves this row as it is, and the trigger is left with a need for a real boundary. A real boundary would be an approval made out of band (signed with a key the seats cannot read, or kept outside the ledger) that spawn and send check | `docs/plans/archive/2026-10-12-org-plan-approval.md` (Design decisions A2; Assumptions); `docs/specs/2026-10-07-org-multi-org-director.md` (FR-7, 7 段目の前半で決めたこと; Security considerations); `internal/org/approve.go` (`approveTripwire`); `internal/org/approval.go` (`planApproval.details`, `checkTaskPlanApproved`); `internal/org/verbs.go` (`sendApprovalErr`, `seatRoleFromEvents`); `internal/org/spawn.go` (`spawnFeatureSeatErr`); commits c08a1a14 and 5596561a |

<!-- RESOLVED 2026-10-16 in feat/org-regate (S1 7b6894ea, S3 5c7f585f, S4 2c3874e2): `ralph org regate --branch <branch>` (`internal/org/regate.go`, `internal/org/regate_merge.go`, `internal/cli/org_regate.go`) brings the branch of a disbanded feature org back to `ready`. In the feature worktree of the branch's latest complete binding it fetches origin's default branch and the branch, refuses a HEAD that is not origin's tip of the branch, merges the default branch's tip in with a merge commit when HEAD lacks it (no rebase, no force push), runs the gate scripts, records `gate_passed` or `gate_failed` with `via=regate` for the binding's org, and pushes a passed merge commit without force. merge-order's Details name it instead of `ralph org gate` for a branch whose org is not running, the `/org` skill's merge してよい順 replaces the manual rebase-and-push path with it, and the `/director` skill runs it one branch at a time when the human asks. Running it without being asked is left to `[org.director].auto_merge` (stage 8a-3a). Row preserved for traceability. -->

| `ralph org merge-order` checks that a PR `gh pr list` returned is from origin's repository by comparing the owner and repo in the PR's `url` with those in `git remote get-url origin`, without case and without the host (`mergeOriginRepo.check` in `internal/org/mergeorder.go`). The check runs only on a PR gh returned: when gh reads another repository that has no PR for the branch, its empty result looks the same as "origin's repository has no PR", and no note is printed. An origin URL with no owner/repo to read (a local path, a `file://` URL, another scheme such as `git+ssh://`) skips the check and uses gh's PRs as they are. A repository renamed on GitHub while origin keeps the old URL fails the check too: git reaches it through GitHub's redirect, but gh's PR urls carry the new name (seen with real gh and an https origin on vuejs/vue-next, renamed on GitHub to vuejs/core, in test cycle 4; an ssh origin URL and a transfer to another owner were not tried). Telling the empty result apart needs another gh call per run, such as `gh repo view --json nameWithOwner`, which also fails when gh is not authenticated. Reading a renamed repository's new name needs the same kind of extra gh call (`gh repo view` with origin's owner/repo), which needs the host that cannot be read from origin's URL, so the note names both fixes instead | A report of merge-order using another host's PRs, or of a fork checkout where merge-order printed no `gh repo set-default` note, or of a renamed repository whose origin keeps the old URL, or stage 8a's director reading merge-order's PRs. The headless director of 8a-2 (org-director-start) follows the same `/director` skill, so it passes merge-order's text on to the human in its herdr pane and merges only when the human asks, and that PR leaves this row as it is; the director part of the trigger moves to automatic merge (stage 8a-3). The automatic merge of 8a-3a (org-auto-merge) acts on a PR with no human in the loop only through `ralph org merge`, which reads origin's host and owner/repo (`mergeOriginFromURL`, in `readOrigin` of `internal/org/merge.go`), pins every gh call to them (`-R <host>/<owner>/<repo>`, `gh api --hostname <host>`), and refuses a PR whose url names another host or owner/repo, the same owner/repo on another host included (plan D10), so this gap does not reach a merge; merge-order's own gh calls are unchanged (plan Non-goals), so the row stays for merge-order's classes and notes | `docs/plans/archive/2026-10-14-org-merge-order.md` (C5); `docs/plans/archive/2026-10-17-org-auto-merge.md` (D10, Non-goals); `docs/reports/cross-review-triage-org-merge-order.md` (the ACTION_REQUIRED findings about the repository check: cross-review cycle 1's, gh reads another repository than origin's, and cycle 3's, a renamed repository; the file puts the latest cycle first, so find them by their content); `internal/org/mergeorder.go` (`lookupMergePRs`, `mergeOriginRepo`, `mergeRepoFromOriginURL`, `mergeRepoFromPRURL`); `docs/reports/test-2026-10-14-org-merge-order.md` (section Cycle 4, the check on a repository renamed on GitHub); `docs/evidence/test-2026-10-14-org-merge-order-c4-rename.txt` (real gh on vuejs/vue-next, renamed to vuejs/core: the note, gh refusing the old note's `gh repo set-default` with the old name, and the PRs used after `git remote set-url origin`) |

For (r): a branch whose fork PRs fill gh's limit took 9 to 10 s of the 15 s gh timeout on a good link (microsoft/vscode `patch-1`, 1000 PRs in 9.64 s), so on a slower link its gh call times out and the branch gets the gh failure note (timed out) in place of the limit note; either way the branch has no PR and a note says so. | The director registration of a promoted session (the `/director` skill, `ralph org director attach`) does not show whether the session is alive. `director.jsonl` (`internal/org/director.go`) keeps a registration `attached` until `ralph org director detach` or a later `attach --replace` ends it. Since stage 8b-2a (org-director-liveness) `ralph org watch --all` checks a headless director each cycle (`CheckDirector` in `internal/org/director_check.go`, `director-alive.json`), and since stage 8b-2b (org-director-wait) a promoted session that waits with `ralph org wait --inbox --director <id>` has the mark of that wait (`director-wait.json`, `internal/org/director_wait.go`): the `wait:` line of `ralph org director status` and the `director:` line of `ralph status` show when its wait (pid N) started and last wrote its mark, adding that it has not written it since when that is more than 60 s old, or when and how it ended. What remains: the mark says what the wait wrote, not that the session runs (a wait that outlived its session keeps writing it, the next row); and a promoted session that uses push only, or waits without `--director`, has no mark, so its line says only that the registration does not tell whether its session is still running and that there is no mark | A human who reads `attached` may wait on a director that is gone: with no mark nothing says so, and a leaked wait's fresh mark can look like a director still waiting. What still leaks depends on the runner. A wait run with the Monitor tool (the `/director` skill's Claude Code form since stage 8b-2c) is stopped with SIGTERM by every session end claude handles (`/exit`'s default "Exit and stop tasks", "Move to background and exit", a pane close, and the Monitor's own expiry at its `timeout_ms`), and outlives its session only when claude is killed (SIGKILL, a crash); the Monitor experiment checked each of these with a shell script, and the live check checked the default exit, the Monitor's expiry, and a SIGKILL with a real wait. A wait run with the Bash tool's `run_in_background` (the skill's alternative) also outlives "Move to background and exit" (and a later `claude stop` of that session). Codex's wait is unchanged and was not checked in this stage: its parent is codex's app-server daemon, which in stage 8b-2a's live check kept a turn's background wait after the director was stopped. How long: the skill now bounds the Claude Code wait to 25 minutes (`--timeout-ms 1500000`, under a Monitor `timeout_ms` of 1800000) and codex's to 300 s (`--timeout-ms 300000`); the command still accepts an unbounded wait, which no copy of the skill runs any more. In the live check's (d), a wait with `--timeout-ms 40000` left running by a SIGKILL of claude ended with `end` timeout 40 s after it started, and its `zsh -c` shell ended with it. Within its bound a leaked wait keeps rewriting `director-wait.json` every 30 s until a new `--director` wait of the same registration takes the mark over (its `wait_id`, under `director-wait.lock`), so the `wait:` line and a promoted session's `director:` line keep saying that the wait (pid N) started at t and last wrote its mark at t2 while its session is gone. It ends at its timeout, with exit 4 or 5, or at the first open inbox item, which it prints to no one and does not ack: the item stays open, the next wait returns it, and if no director acks it, it reaches the human at the ack deadline. Once a new wait owns the mark, the leaked one writes it no more and keeps waiting. The hole that remains: when that new wait is then killed with SIGKILL, the leaked wait goes on waiting without writing, and the mark keeps the killed wait's old `seen_at`; this is accepted, since two waits for one director are abnormal already. The wait still does not watch its owner: its parent is the `zsh -c` shell that claude runs it in, which outlives a SIGKILL of claude and is reparented to pid 1, so the wait's own parent pid does not change; how many levels lie between the wait and claude depends on how the shell wraps the command (fork or exec); and codex's wait's parent is a daemon, so the parent chain tells nothing there. The owner rule keeps the later wait's writes, so two waits do not corrupt the mark | The consult (consult-plan-promoted, 2026-10-19) split it off as part B of stage 8b-2b (org-director-wait, Non-goals; D7), until each driver's process chain was checked. "$1"` (`digest_body`), so a relative name whose part before a `=` is an awk variable name, such as `a=b.md` or `x_1=y.md`, is taken as an awk variable assignment, and awk reads stdin instead of the file. `[ -f "$1" ]` passes first, so nothing fails: with an empty stdin it prints `e3b0c44298fc` (the SHA-256 of nothing) and exits 0. `./a=b.md`, `sub/a=b.md`, an absolute path, and a name that starts with a digit (`2026-10-15-a=b.md`) are read as files. Each listed one is a case `lint` handles correctly that no test line tells from the mutant, and each was shown in scratch to fail once its fixture is added. L1e: the `<!--` branch in the main loop of `lint_page` removed, so a comment ends at its first `>`: a page with `<!-- a > b <figure class="easy" id="overview"> -->` before figure 1 passes at HEAD and gets an L1 line (and, since the cycle-2 nested-figure check, one more L6 line) from the mutant. L2c: the floor of 1 lowered to 0: a page with no easy figure gets L1 anyway, so the exit code stays 1, and the older-form page and the empty file check only the L1 line, not `L2: 0 easy figures`. L4f: an `aria-label` on a tag other than `<svg>` accepted: every L4 fixture puts it on the `<svg>`. L5b: two `<section class="before">` accepted: no fixture has two. O6: the once-per-line check on `SEEN` in `add` removed: no fixture repeats a word or a `mono` class. O7: a repeated attribute read last instead of first (`parse_attrs`; S1 chose first wins): no fixture repeats one. O9: a directory passed to `lint` still read: awk exits 2, `lint failed: awk exited 2`, exit 1 as before, and the suite checks only the exit code, so it needs an empty-stdout and a `not found or not readable` stderr assertion. O11: an awk exit other than 0 or 1 returned as 0: no fixture runs a failing awk (a fake `awk` that only runs `exit 2`, first on `PATH`, would). B3: the `tag_space()` call in the comment branch of `lint_page` removed, so a comment no longer splits a word: no line pins that it does; a page with `<p>internal/<!-- c -->org</p>` in the foot of figure 1 passes at HEAD and gets `overview: L3: path-like text: internal/org` from the mutant. (c) Two instructions that depend on `lint` are skill text with no test: `/plan` step 10.c (an older `plan-visual.sh` whose `--help` has no `lint` line: use the main checkout's copy, or skip and say why) and `/pr` step 4.a (telling the current page form from the older one by `id="detail-overview"`); the suite pins only the `--help` line they key on. (e) Three ways to write a path, which the cycle-2 implementer found and the cycle-2 fix left out of scope, still pass L3 and no test pins them: an HTML comment inside a word (`internal/<!-- -->org`, which a browser draws as `internal/org`; the comment splits the word, see B3), a character reference for the slash (`internal&#47;org`, `internal&sol;org`; `lint` does not decode references), and an upper-case tag name (`<CODE>` is not read as `<code>`, and an upper-case `<TSPAN>` splits a word like any tag outside the list of 10 inline tags; `lint` reads lower-case tag names only, as before). (b) A change that reaches only the template copy is caught by `check-sync.sh` as a difference between the copies, not as a behavior failure. | `run_tests` in `packs/languages/golang/verify.sh` runs `go test ./...` with no `-timeout`, so each package gets `go test`'s 10-minute default, and on the maintainer's Mac `internal/org` and `internal/cli` have come close to it. On 2026-10-19 (org-director-wait) a local `./scripts/run-verify.sh` failed with `panic: test timed out after 10m0s` in `internal/org` (601.9 s, `TestRegate_RefusedBeforeTheFetch` running at the time; `internal/cli` passed in 592.0 s); a later run of nearly the same tree (a split of test files and comment changes) with `GOFLAGS=-timeout=30m` passed, `internal/org` in 328 s and `internal/cli` in 260 s. Why the first run took nearly twice as long is not known (a reviewer subagent was reading the diff beside it, which runs no tests); the stage 8b-2a test report had a run of `internal/org` alone at 597 s (`GOTOOLCHAIN=go1.25.8`, `-v`). The tests that stage added to `internal/org` take about 6 s. CI's verify job (ubuntu-latest) runs the whole of `run-verify.sh` in about 4 minutes. The same PR's last local run-verify, with `GOFLAGS=-timeout=30m` and a load average near 50 from other sessions on the Mac, took 1537 s for `internal/org` and 1472 s for `internal/cli`, inside the 30 minutes. In the trial of stage 9 (2026-10-20, F10) the director's first `ralph org regate --branch fix/doctor-escape`, run with no `GOFLAGS`, failed on time alone under a load average of 45 to 75: `internal/cli` timed out at 601.4 s and `internal/org` at 602.7 s (`panic: test timed out after 10m0s`). The seats' gates had `GOFLAGS=-timeout=30m` from the herdr server's environment and none of them timed out; the second regate, given the same `GOFLAGS`, passed. Neither `ralph org gate` nor `ralph org regate` sets a test timeout, so a gate's result depends on the environment of whoever runs it | A local run-verify can fail on time alone while the code and CI are fine, and a timed-out run says nothing about the tests that did not run. A gate or regate run from a shell without `GOFLAGS` fails the same way (F10) | Raising the timeout in the language pack changes what `ralph init` ships to every project, and finding the slow tests is a change of its own | The next local run-verify that times out, a CI verify job that takes more than 8 minutes, or a gate or regate that times out: find the slowest tests with `go test -json`, or give `run_tests` a `-timeout` | `docs/plans/archive/2026-10-19-org-director-wait.md`; `docs/reports/verify-2026-10-19-org-director-wait.md`; `docs/evidence/org-trial-2026-10-20.md` (Findings, F10; Merges, Regate 1); `docs/evidence/org-trial-2026-10-20/gate-excerpts.md` (`doctor-escape-20261020T062210Z.log`) |

| `truncateForDetails` (`internal/org/verbs.go`) cuts the body in a `sent` event's Details at a byte count (`text[:sendDetailsMaxLen]`, 200 bytes), which can split a multi-byte character. In the stage 9 trial (F12) four `sent` lines with Japanese bodies end in one or two JSON escapes of U+FFFD (one per byte of the split character) before `...(truncated)`, and the two org reports, which print those Details, hold the U+FFFD character itself. Nothing in CI scans for U+FFFD: the mojibake check (`.claude/hooks/check_mojibake.sh`) runs only as a hook on Edit and Write, so a committed org report with it passes | A reader of the manifest or of an org report sees a broken last character in a truncated Japanese body, and a check for U+FFFD (such as the mojibake hook on a file an agent edits) flags text that ralph wrote | Found in the trial's snapshot; the fix is a code change (trial plan Non-goals) | The next change to `truncateForDetails` or to the `sent` event: cut at a rune boundary (for example step back while `!utf8.RuneStart(text[n])`), with a test whose body has a multi-byte character across byte 200 | `docs/evidence/org-trial-2026-10-20.md` (Snapshot; Findings, F12); `docs/evidence/org-trial-2026-10-20/manifest.jsonl` (the `sent` events at 04:09:23Z, 04:44:51Z, 04:58:07Z, and 05:37:13Z); `docs/evidence/org-trial-2026-10-20/reports/`; `docs/plans/archive/2026-10-20-org-trial.md` (Non-goals); `internal/org/verbs.go` (`truncateForDetails`, `sendDetailsMaxLen`) |
| The watchdog's escalation path drops the notification's error: `escalateAlert` (`internal/org/watch.go`) runs `_ = w.escalateFn(...)`, as `realEscalate`'s doc means it to (an `osascript` failure is a silent no-op), so nothing records whether a notification went out.
